"[EXCERPT extracted-character range 0:4000]\n# K-SYS-003：位置、Mask 与缓存：同一可见性关系的三种表示\n\n缓存中的内容必须对应模型定义的逻辑位置；mask 决定读取集合，物理布局决定这些内容放在哪里。三者通过显式映射共同实现 attention，不能用张量长度、因果标志或槽号中的任意一个代替其余关系。\n\n## 对象、坐标与共同函数\n\n本章先限定为固定参数、推理态、无 attention dropout 的因果 self-attention。有效分数与 V 有限。普通全注意力允许 query 读取同一请求中截至自身的所有有效 key；模型原生窗口另加窗口下界。cross-attention、双向注意力、树形投机与递归状态需要各自的依赖关系，不能直接继承这个因果集合。\n\n必须分别表达以下对象：\n\n| 符号或状态 | 含义 |\n|---|---|\n| t、j | 从零开始的逻辑 token 序号，决定因果先后；padding 不必占用逻辑 token 序号 |\n| π(t) | 模型位置编码使用的坐标；本章公式采用标量，模型也可能使用多轴坐标 |\n| i、u | 当前 Q 的局部行号与 K 张量的局部列号；不天然等于 t、j |\n| valid(u)、logical(u) | 列是否有效，以及有效列承载的逻辑位置；未填充容量不能被当作有效历史 |\n| locate(l,j) | 第 l 层逻辑位置 j 到物理页、槽和表示元数据的映射 |\n| c、A | 所有必要层已提交 KV 的连续终点，以及已确认输入终点，满足 0≤c≤A；已知 token 不等于已有 KV |\n| binding、generation | 本次请求授权读取的内容身份，以及对象或尝试代次；合法共享前缀允许不同请求绑定同一不可变对象 |\n\n输入为本层隐藏状态、Q/K/V 投影与 head 映射、位置规则、有效性和可见性规则，以及带身份、位置、表示和完成依赖的旧缓存。输出为各 query 的 attention、新位置的 KV 和相应提交状态。高层 KV 来源于该层输入；不能从原始 token 一次生成所有层 KV。\n\n对 query head h，令 ρ(h) 为所读 KV head，q̄、k̄ 为位置变换前向量，q、k 为实际参加点积的向量。有效 query t 的合法集合为\n\nAₜ={j：j 属于本请求绑定的有效输入，0≤j≤t}。\n\n普通窗口宽 W≥1，且 W 包含当前 token 时，再要求 j≥max(0,t−W+1)。窗口上下界使用逻辑序号；不能因为 π(t) 不连续或不单调，就改用位置编码坐标比较因果先后。sink/global 等附加规则应按集合并集去重。\n\n对缩放 a、位置偏置 b，定义\n\nzₜⱼₕ=a·qₜₕ·kⱼ,ρ(h)+bₜⱼₕ，\n\nOₜₕ=Σⱼ∈Aₜ exp(zₜⱼₕ−mₜₕ)Vⱼ,ρ(h) / Σⱼ∈Aₜ exp(zₜⱼₕ−mₜₕ)，\n\nmₜₕ=maxⱼ∈Aₜ zₜⱼₕ。\n\n默认缩放常取 1/√dₖ，但由模型合同固定。不可见位置不进入分子或分母。将分数乘零会让其指数变为 1，并非屏蔽。例如合法与非法位置的分数都为 0、V 分别为 0 和 2，正确输出为 0，乘零后的错误输出为 1。\n\n普通因果 self-attention 的有效 query 应至少看见自身。若出现空集合，先检查缺失当前 KV、有效性或对齐错误。模型或接口确实允许空行时，应明确返回零、错误或其他值；不能计算空最大值，再让全为负无穷的 softmax 自行决定。补齐出来的无效 query 可以绕过归约并输出约定值，但不得推进请求状态。\n\n## 从逻辑关系推导矩阵对齐\n\n假设 Q 行 i 对应 t=q₀+i，K 列 u 对应 j=k₀+u，且两侧都是连续有效区间，则因果条件为\n\nu≤i+q₀−k₀。\n\n左上三角 u≤i 仅在 q₀=k₀ 时表达这个关系。若 Q 是 K 的连续尾部，两者结束于同一逻辑终点，Q 长 L、K 长 S，则 q₀−k₀=S−L，得到右下对齐 u≤i+S−L。右下对齐的合法性来自共同终点，不能仅由 L≠S 推断。\n\n历史已有 5 个有效 token，本轮 Q 长 2，对应位置 5、6，最终有效 K 长 7。两行集合分别为 {0,…,5}、{0,…,6}。左上三角只提供 {0}、{0,1}，shape 完全正确却计算了另一个函数。\n\n若 K 张量前面有 3 个无效 padding 列，有效逻辑位置 j 位于列 u=j+3，则两行的有效可见列分别为 3…8、3…9。即使右下对齐碰巧给出 u≤8、u≤9，也仍需排除列 0…2。π(5)、π(6) 不应仅因为多了 3 列 padding 就自动增加 3。\n\n右 padding、静态预分配尾部、压紧 batch、任意页排列或稀疏保留通常破坏上述连续列假设。此时应先通过列描述符恢复请求归属、有效性和 logical(u)，再应用模型可见性。请求压紧只改变执行行号；共享前缀只改变对象归属与引用；二者都不能重新编号逻辑 token。\n\n固定版本的 [SDPA 说明](../sources/S-SDPA.md) 对非方形因果模式采用左上对齐，布尔 true 表示参与；该版本不允许因果标志与显式 mask 同时设置。[FlashAttention 说明](../sources/S-FA-IMPL.md) 记录 2.1 将非方形因果对齐改为右下。接入时必须把期望集合与实际接口逐行对照。同名 causal 参数不构成函数等价证明。\n\n## RoPE：位置进入数值内容的时刻\n\n对一对通道 (x₀,x₁)，频率 θ 的旋转为\n\nR(pθ)(x₀,x₁)=(x₀cos(pθ)−x₁sin(pθ)，x₀sin(pθ)+x₁cos(pθ))。\n\n对偶数旋转维 dᵣ，将规定的通道对分别旋转，其余通道保持原值。相邻配对与半区配对是不同布局合同，必须与投影输出的排列一致。不能只改一个配对开关而假定同一权重仍实现原函数。\n\n固定频率、配对与坐标规则时，令 qₜ=R(π(t))q̄ₜ、kⱼ=R(π(j))k̄ⱼ。正交关系给出\n\nqₜᵀkⱼ=q̄ₜᵀR(π(j)−π(t))k̄ⱼ。\n\n右侧还可按每对通道直接展开为\n\ncos(δθ)(q̄₀k̄₀+q̄₁k̄₁)+sin(δθ)(q̄₁k̄₀−q̄₀k̄₁)，δ=π(j)−π(t)。\n\n这提供了不复用实际旋转函数的检查参考，也明确了旋转符号。RoPE 给出相对位置内积关系，不保证任意 token 对的 attention 权重随距离单调下降，也不保证任意长上下文质量。\n\n缓存可以保存两种不同对象：\n\n- 已旋转 K：写入时计算 R(π(j))k̄ⱼ；消费时直接读取，不能再次旋转。新 query 仍需按自己的 π(t) 旋转。\n- 未旋转 K：写入 k̄ⱼ并保存正确坐标；消费时在临时 tile 或其他受控路径中旋转，再参与点积。必须说明旋转在哪个粒度上复用，不能每次读取时原地旋转共享缓存。\n\n两条路径在相同输入与实数运算中等价。有限精度下，旋转、存储舍入和归约顺序可能产生误差。两种表示不能仅凭 shape 相同直接互换。V 是否带位置变换也由模型规定；本章的普通 RoPE 算例仅旋转 Q/K。\n\n若只改变物理页排列，逻辑位置和 K 数值应保持不变。若希望把坐标 p 改为 p′，且确知底层未旋转向量不变、变换可逆，则在实数中可使用 K′=R新(p′)R旧(p)ᵀK。这个局部恒等式不证明整个多层模型缓存可复用：修改位置规则可能已经改变前层 attention 和高层未旋转 K/V。量化也通常不可逆。没有完整依赖证明时，应重算受影响状态。\n\n对于仅使用固定 RoPE、无其他绝对位置依赖的局部函数，同时给所有 Q/K 坐标增加同一常数会保持相对内积；这可作受限变形检查。只平移新 query、保留旧 K 的坐标，或把窗口槽号当坐标，都不满足该条件。不能把局部平移性质推广成任意模型的前缀搬移许可。\n\n## 增量执行的完整数据流与提交\n\n下面选择每请求最多一轮推进在途、旧已发布状态只读、新块使用独立 staging 的基线。它覆盖全注意力与普通窗口；共享对象采用私有尾区或写时复制。该选择便于说明正确性，具体引擎可以使用具有等价依赖保证的融合路径。\n\n```pseudocode\nADVANCE(request, q)\n输入：已确认 token[c:c+q]；逐层参数；位置/可见性/表示合同\n      旧已发布视图、对象身份和代次；可用容量与完成依赖\n输出：q 行模型结果、新提交终点 c+q，或不推进 c 的失败\n\n核对请求仍有效、无其他推进在途、0≤c≤c+q≤A\n若 q=0：返回空结果，不分配、不写入、不改变 c\n核对位置范围、表示兼容性及本轮全部必需输入\n计算各层各 query 的合法集合和旧历史读取并集\n原子取得旧对象读取责任；核对身份、逻辑标签和表示\n预留新 staging、目标写区、必要 COW 和 workspace\n任一步失败：释放尚未产生物理使用的责任，返回失败\n\nX ← token[c:c+q] 的模型输入表示\nfor l 从第一层到最后一层：\n    按模型顺序，从 X 计算本层 Q、未旋转 K 和 V\n    按 π(c+i) 旋转每行 Q\n    若缓存合同为已旋转 K：旋转新增 K 一次\n    在本层独立 staging 保存全部新增 K/V 及位置、表示元数据\n    候选对象 ← 已取得读取权的旧对象 ∪ 本层新 staging\n    对每个 query t=c+i：\n        从候选中选择恰好属于 A_t 的对象；每个逻辑 k\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 8000:10071]\n56 KiB 内容读写，合计 512 KiB，尚不含 attention 读取、metadata 或 staging。P₂ 的额外分配为 512 KiB，不能只按其三个有效 token 估算。旧 P₁ 仍被前缀或在途读者持有时不能扣除；B 分叉还需自己的私有页。\n\n同例 q=2、H=5，E=13；若 Hq=32，则每层 QK/PV 主乘加为 2·32·256·13=212,992 FLOP。这个量不包括投影，也不是时延预测。若 q=128、S=8192，单个无 head 复制的显式 mask 有 1,048,576 个元素，按每布尔 1 B 为 1 MiB，按 FP32 加性偏置为 4 MiB；广播能否保持视图而不物化要按实际路径核对。\n\n串行路径可写 T=T准备+T复制/转换+T设备+T提交。存在重叠时，应沿生产者到消费者的依赖图取关键路径，并检查带宽等共享资源，不能把所有累计耗时相加，也不能只取最快 kernel 作为完整调用成本。\n\n## 条件改变后保留什么\n\n| 变化 | 可以保留的关系 | 需要调整或放弃的部分 |\n|---|---|---|\n| 只改变 padding、batch 顺序、物理页排列 | 输入与模型函数、逻辑位置、合法集合 | 重建执行行列和地址映射；检查 inactive 槽及归属 |\n| 改变 chunk 划分 | 普通确定性因果模型的逻辑依赖 | staging、历史重读、准备次数及浮点归约；量化封组策略另作合同 |\n| 窗口缩小 | 新窗口仍允许的状态内容 | 只在模型定义或质量合同允许时改变函数；等待读者结束再回收 |\n| 窗口扩大 | 仍有合法副本的旧位置 | 已覆盖历史无法靠改长度恢复，应重算或取回副本 |\n| RoPE 配对、频率、缩放或输入起点改变 | 仍完全不受影响的依赖 | 重新证明整个状态依赖；局部旋转公式不能代替多层证明 |\n| raw K 与 rotated K 后端切换 | 已证明相同的未旋转内容与坐标 | 转换、舍入、表示标志和读写路径；量化往返通常不可逆 |\n| 只改变布局或迁移设备 | 内容与位置语义 | 元数据、stride、head 排列、ready 依赖与峰值；含 scale 一起转换 |\n| 模型、adapter、前缀或外部特征改变 | 无关请求的旧不可变对象 | 旧命中资格通常失效；同 token 数、同 shape 不足以复用 |\n| 采用近似裁剪或重要 token 保留 | 被保留对象的身份与位置 | 原全 attention 函数已经改变，增加任务质量参考 |\n\n短历史、小 q 时，显式小 mask 与简单路径可能比复杂计划便宜。长历史时，结构参数或融合 mask 更容易摊薄准备与驻留，但仅在能够准确表达全部可见性时使用。原生窗口降低有效对数并不保证执行 tile 和 DRAM 事务同比减少。未旋转 K 若被多步重复旋转可能不合算；旋转后缓存若带来迁移格式不兼容，也可能使整体成本反转。选择需要相同函数、输入、计时边界与容量约束。\n\n## 竞争解释、负对照与检查边界\n\n先检查具体可见关系，再比较输出。只检查可见对数、shape 或首 token 都可能漏错：两个集合可以大小相等但成员不同；首 token 可能尚未读取迁移或量化后的历史；纯 RoPE 的共同坐标平移还可能掩盖单一 padding 样例的问题。\n\n| 观察 | 竞争解释 | 区分实验及反证后的修正 |\n|---|---|---|\n| 方形 prefill 正确，增量失败 | 非方形左上对齐；本块位置重置；旧 K 重复旋转 | 固定 H=5、q=2，先比较逐行逻辑集合，再比较原始与旋转后 K；集合错先修 mask，集合对再查位置数值 |\n| padding 后结果变化 | 无效列进入分母；位置整体偏移；请求行路由错误 | 同一输入独立、左补齐、混批执行，改变 padding 内 V 为大有限 poison；另核对每个有效 token 的坐标和归属 |\n| chunk 跨窗口时失败 | 提前覆盖；当前 key 重复；窗口端点错 | 执行 W=4、[5,8) 轨迹，记录每行位置标签与贡献次数；等分数参考为 3.5、4.5、5.5，分别注入覆盖和重复贡献 |\n| 非整页命中后出错 | 尾页漏复制；有效长度扩到未填槽；共享页原地改写 | 新页以 poison 初始化，独立读取旧物理尾槽再复制；检查 A、B 和旧前缀的字节与后续输出，不能用全量重打包自动补好遗漏 |\n| 迁移后标签正确但数值错 | raw/rotated 合同不匹配；尺度缺失；head/通道排列错误 | 固定逻辑输入，分别比较位置处理、解码后的 K/V 和逐行输出；若首次差异发生在解码前，修表示而不是放宽输出容差 |\n| 只在取消或并发时错 | 早回收；迟到旧写；共享 workspace 覆盖 | 控制最后使用与完成顺序，复用槽后再放行旧事件；需要真实平台验证物理可见性，C", "excerpt_ranges": [[0, "Ranked 4000-character windows by current entry title/tags/questions and algorithm/mechanism keywords; ties retain source order."}, {"path": "knowledge/system-semantics/evidence-and-independent-checks.md", "sha256": "50b7c97de928be322713ef149fb734c0c20acda17c4a321c46a198b0019831be", "reading_scope": "full", "category": "related_chapter", "content": "# K-SYS-006：工程认识如何成为可迁移的专家知识\n\n知识的增量是对系统机制与设计关系的新增解释；运行结果为这些解释提供事实、区分和修正。\n\n## 从现象走到解释\n\n“改参数后更快”记录一个现象；“减少了哪些重复数据流、为何该运行点受到它限制”才形成可迁移解释。完整关系包含对象、依赖、干预、结果和竞争解释。例如开启前缀缓存后 TTFT 下降，可由少算 prompt、请求排队变化或预热差异导致，单个平均数不能区分。\n\n系统知识可以来自算法定义、接口契约、代数推导和实际实验。来源支持一个机制存在，目标运行支持该机制在某组条件下的具体表现。将两者连起来，需要说明模型结构、硬件路径和 workload 如何满足推导前提。\n\n## 什么值得进入正文\n\n值得积累的是更准确的成本关系、更清晰的状态不变量、新发现的适用分支、可复现的反例及其原因，或原本分开的机制怎样组合。一次路径错误、固定文件名和临时命令通常属于运行记录；若它揭示更普遍的版本/所有权问题，再提炼其机制。\n\n例如一次 graph 重放串请求的经历，可以推导“稳定地址与内容代次是两项独立契约”，并说明双缓冲何时足够、三个在途代次为什么还会冲突。这比增加一句“注意同步”提供更多设计能力。\n\n## 独立核对的作用\n\n实现者解释可能与其代码共享同一错误假设。诊断从任务函数、独立参考和原始执行观察出发，检验候选解释的可区分预测。局部 kernel 数值一致支持该 kernel 的某些输入范围，端到端 SLO 支持整个路径的一个运行点，各自都不能替代另一层证据。\n\nRSI 的下一次使用是继续检验和扩展理解的机会。成功可能提示条件更广，失败可能揭示遗漏机制，也可能只是代码没有遵循知识。将后一种失败错误地当作知识反例，会让系统丢弃正确原理；将任务整体成功给所有被读条目记功，则会强化无效经验。\n\n## 修订应保留推导能力\n\n更新一个章节时，保留仍成立的对象模型、公式、布局和设计分支，把新认识放到它改变的地方。修订理由解释发生了何种认识变化。历史用于理解演化轨迹；正文应保持可独立阅读，而不是不断堆积时间顺序的日志。\n\n## 把论断定位到它真正得到支持的位置\n\n一篇论文可以支持一种算法定义，却未必支持某个 GPU、负载或生产配置上的速度判断。知识正文中的关键论断应能够追溯到固定来源的具体机制段、接口条款、推导前提或对应观测。只在章节末列出十个来源，不能说明哪个来源承担了哪个结论。\n\n例如“分层存储可以扩大可驻留模型范围”与“本任务通过卸载降低尾延迟”是两个论断。前者可由容量分配关系解释，后者还需要排队、传输、预取和并发测量。将实验条件从低并发改为高并发后，容量关系仍成立，性能结论却可能反转。修订应改变受影响的论断范围，而非把整篇标成全部正确或全部错误。\n\n来源的实际版本、编辑日期、实验观察日期分别记录。今天可以依据截止前的来源整理新的代数推导；今天观察到的工程事实应保留今天的日期，不能借引用旧论文把新观察放回历史 seed。\n\n## 冲突先分辨函数、条件与证据层次\n\n两个来源看似冲突时，先比较其目标函数与状态：吞吐和首 token 延迟、无损与近似、热缓存与冷启动、单请求与开放到达都可能不同。随后比较硬件拓扑、模型形状、实现成熟度和测量边界。只有共同条件下的互斥预测，才构成需要裁决的机制冲突。\n\n一种有效的反例应能说明被反驳的具体量词。一个短请求上卸载更慢，可以反驳“卸载总会更快”，不能反驳“卸载在容量不足时提供可执行路径”。若失败来自实现违反已写明的所有权条件，应记录实现偏离与诊断证据，不能据此删除正确的资源回收原理。\n\n## 跨机制组合需要新的核对\n\n分别验证前缀缓存、量化、异步和迁移，不代表其组合已经验证。组合可能改变身份、恢复位置、scale 生存期、消费者完成点和随机状态归属。可沿共同边界组织检查：先确认各组件输入输出契约，再构造会触发交互的最小状态，最后在真实负载上验证资源和 SLO。\n\n例如将量化 KV 迁到另一个实例，必须同时验证编码版本与元数据、分片布局、传输完整范围、目标可见性及旧实例的回收权。只对比传输字节哈希不能证明模型消费方式相同；只对比一次输出也不能证明取消或重试时不会释放在途状态。\n\n## 怎样检验知识对设计过程的帮助\n\n读取记录证明某个版本被访问，不能证明内容被理解、正确使用或造成收益。要研究知识本身的贡献，可以固定模型、任务族、资源、预算与评价规则，比较不同知识条件，并按任务或随机种子配对。生成过程存在随机性，单次最优结果不能代替多次结果分布。\n\n评价同时覆盖设计正确性、完成率、满足质量/SLO 的有效产出、试验与实现成本，以及错误类型。需要区分知识帮助发现方案、缩小错误分支和改善最终引擎三种作用。对多轮搜索后的获胜方案，独立保留任务或轨迹用于最终评价；[实验统计](../tools/statistical-experiments.md) 解释搜索选择偏差和配对关系。\n\n如果新增知识只减少错误尝试而没有提高最终吞吐，它仍可能改善构建效率，但结论应写在相应指标上。若差异在独立任务上消失，应收窄迁移范围，不能用一次被选中的成功运行替所有章节记功。\n\n<!-- infraagents:provenance -->\n\n## 来源与推导\n\n- [S-ACE：Agentic Context Engineering: "knowledge/tools/timeline-attribution.md", "sha256": "cf4b01a8ca5cb35e653b65fbe2bc018d3ae5cc42788a77688524fb29c7da817d", "reading_scope": "full", "category": "related_chapter", "content": "# K-TOOLS-002：时间线分析：从请求路径定位执行瓶颈\n\n时间线分析先建立关联和依赖，再用区间与关键路径解释延迟；热点累计时长只是入口。\n\n## 由外向内的对象关系\n\n一个慢请求对应若干调度批，一个批对应主机准备与设备执行，一个 forward 对应多个 kernel、复制和通信。NVTX/框架范围帮助定位阶段，API/kernel correlation 帮助连接提交与执行，request/batch 元数据连接业务身份。所有关系都要在进程和设备作用域内解释。\n\n## 三种时间线形态\n\nGPU 有长空洞且 CPU 忙于准备，支持主机供给不足；GPU 连续忙但单请求排队长，支持容量或调度问题；多个 stream 各自有工作却在同步点等待，支持依赖或负载不均衡。它们可能同时出现，且随并发变化。\n\n对一个同步 API，长 host 时长可能只是等待之前工作；改这个调用位置可能转移等待而不减少总路径。对频繁小 kernel，间隙可能来自 host launch；融合或 graph 的预测是减少这些间隙，而不是让原 kernel 本身一定变短。\n\n## 聚合后回到原始事件\n\n按 kernel 名汇总找候选热点，再选择与慢请求关联的具体实例，保留开始/结束、stream、shape 和调用上下文。重叠区间先求并集再讨论设备忙碌时间；不同设备累计时间不能直接当墙钟。\n\n采样窗口要包含问题触发前的状态与完成后的结果。只截中间 5 ms 可能看不见队列形成或缓存回收原因。最终无 profiler 的服务测量确认收益，短 trace 用来解释机制，二者互为补充。\n\n## 跨主机和 I/O 队列的时间线\n\n一段加载或迁移至少有请求创建、容量就绪、提交、传输完成、目标可见和消费几个事件。等待容量与等待传输属于不同资源；NIC 报告本地完成也不等于远端应用已消费。跨机可见事件的关联要依赖请求/对象/代次，时间轴相减则另需校准与误差界限。\n\n块 I/O 还可能在软件队列、派发队列和设备服务之间等待。把文件调用整体画成“磁盘执行”会把调度、页缓存和唤醒开销混在一起；把 GPU copy 区间单独画完，也可能漏掉此前等待 pinned buffer 或 CPU 格式转换的时间。瓶颈解释应覆盖消费者真正等待的完整范围。\n\n## 一组正反归因算例\n\n设每步需要的权重读取为 10 ms、复制为 4 ms、计算为 6 ms。在完全串行且无额外成本的假设下，一步为 20 ms。若双缓冲允许下一步读取与当前计算重叠，稳态吞吐受最长阶段及共享资源约束；若读取和复制还必须串行争用同一个 staging slot，不能直接声称稳态为 10 ms。\n\n一种支持预取有效的证据组合是：消费者等待减少、相同有效工作对应的物理读取不增加、驻留增加处于预算内，且无采集的端到端测量改善。相反，只观察到异步 API 更早返回，而完成事件和输出时间不变，支持的是等待位置移动。若低并发改善而高并发退化，应进一步检查预取占用容量与排队干扰，不能只保留改善区间。\n\n## 共享工作怎样分配成本\n\n一个批次的矩阵计算可能同时推进多个请求；一次远端前缀读取也可能服务多个会话。物理时间线中只出现一次工作的持续时间，逻辑请求各自的延迟却都可能受到它影响。把 8 ms 的共享阶段记入十个请求的等待解释是合理的，随后把这十份 8 ms 加成设备忙碌 80 ms 则改变了统计对象。\n\n资源成本可以按 token、字节或实际计算量分摊，但必须声明分摊规则；任何分摊都不替代真实关键路径。比较调度方案时同时报告物理资源使用、请求分布和满足 SLO 的有效完成量，避免共享率提高却让长尾等待更差。\n\n## 反事实预测与最终确认\n\n对热点提出明确预测：若减少历史复制是原因，则相同上下文下复制字节随步数的增长应减弱；若降低调度开销是原因，host 准备间隙应缩短，而单 kernel 的计算量未必变化。再通过保持其他条件的候选对照观察预测是否出现。\n\n只在 profiler 下出现的收益可能来自工具改变并发、cache 或 clock 状态。最终回到正常运行点验证；若性能改善而预测没有成立，应修正因果解释，即使该候选仍然值得保留。\n\n<!-- infraagents:provenance -->\n\n## 来源与推导\n\n- [S-NSYS：Nsight Systems 2024.6 — User Guide](../sources/S-NSYS.md)：Nsight Systems 2024.6 用户指南描述 host "knowledge/algorithms/kv-offload-prefetch.md", "sha256": "a7088eba5c78cd9e96cf011d61946ad7dec4ecb6378ac16e7f2fc3820af38301", "reading_scope": "full", "category": "related_chapter", "content": "# K-ALG-018：KV 卸载与预取：读集合、恢复点与迁移预算\n\nKV 卸载需要同时回答存储在哪里、下一步 attention 会读哪些状态，以及何时能够恢复推进。释放显存、减少重复 prefill 和降低 decode 流量是不同目标，采用的机制也不同。\n\n## 先区分三类使用\n\n暂停请求的 KV 可以换出后在恢复时读回；跨请求前缀缓存可以保存在更低层，命中后恢复；活跃 decode 的历史 KV 则可能每一步都被读取。前两种主要支付切换或命中成本，第三种可能持续支付整段历史的传输。不能把低频前缀缓存的收益外推为频繁活跃卸载的收益。\n\n可见集合由模型决定：全 attention、滑动窗口、MLA 和 hybrid state 具有不同状态表示与读取范围。把一部分 KV 放到 SSD 并不会让模型不再需要它；主动裁剪或近似选择历史属于另一个质量契约。混合模型恢复还要使其他状态与 KV 对齐到共同的逻辑前缀。\n\n## 布局与目的缓冲\n\n按层读取便于与层计算流水，按 token 块管理便于共享与局部替换，按 head/并行分片存放则可能匹配执行器。存储格式与设备布局不同会引入 scatter/gather、解压或转置；代价和临时副本需要计入。共同前缀的物理块不能按每个引用重复换出，也不能因取消一个请求就删除其他请求仍需要的副本。\n\n已封闭前缀与正在追加的尾块分开管理。导出尾块时固定一个有效范围与代次；恢复后尚未写入的空槽不属于有效 KV。量化表示还必须迁移对应 scale/zero point 和分组布局。仅有一个文件长度或哈希不能替代模型版本、位置与布局的语义检查。\n\n## 搬运和重算的比较\n\n令恢复状态为 D 字节，可用带宽 B，恢复时延至少包含排队+D/B+转换+同步；重算需要从持有的 token/特征及必要恢复点重新执行，时间为 Tᵣ。只有它们恢复同一语义边界时才可比较。token 本身不足以恢复任意模型状态、随机分支或已经提交的外部协议，所需额外状态参见 [分布式状态迁移](../system-semantics/distributed-state-transfer.md)。\n\n设定一个待恢复前缀占 512 MiB，主机到设备有效带宽 16 GiB/s，则纯传输下界为 31.25 ms。若同一目标形态的重算成本假设为 20 ms，恢复不能仅凭缓存命中被判为更快；若重算为 200 ms，传输路线才有潜在优势，仍需计入排队和转换。从 2 GiB/s 的 SSD 读取同样数据，仅读取下界就为 250 ms，结论会再次变化。这里的重算数值是比较设定，实际策略必须校准。\n\n## 逐层预取与持续带宽\n\n层 l 的 KV 被消费前，目的空间、数据传输和布局转换都要完成。可以在前面若干层计算时预取，但下一层提前量由计算区间决定，不能只按层号相差固定个数保证。双缓冲减少等待的前提是前一个消费者已结束且所需数据已就绪；更深缓冲会挤占当前活跃 KV 的容量。\n\n对 L 层、Bᵣ 个请求、历史 N、Hkv 个 head、维度 d、s 字节的普通全 attention，完整历史主项为 D=2LBᵣNHkvds。如果这些状态每步都经过同一 H2D 链路，D/B链路 是每步下界。设定 L=32、Bᵣ=8、N=4096、Hkv=8、d=128、s=2，D=4 GiB；16 GiB/s 链路的下界为 250 ms。分页可以组织这笔流量，却不会自动消除它。可比较把部分 attention 计算留在 CPU，见 [异构执行](heterogeneous-execution.md)。\n\n## 发布、取消与部分失败\n\n恢复请求先保留目的块和对应模型状态版本，逐范围接收完成结果；只有所需集合齐备才发布新的可执行视图。部分命中可以复用正确前缀并计算后缀，但不能拼接不同输入或模型版本的状态。若其中一层读取失败，不能让其他层的新映射独自进入下一次 forward。\n\n取消后停止新的预取，丢弃业务输出，但等待已发起的 I/O/DMA/设备读结束再释放缓冲。迟到完成按原请求、对象和代次结算，不能覆盖新分配的页。源副本的删除与请求终态分开判断，保证失败或取消后至少保留策略要求的恢复能力。\n\n## 区分实验和选择反转\n\n用冷缓存、DRAM 命中、SSD 命中、部分前缀和长历史活跃请求分别对照全驻留/卸载/重算。测真正避免的计算、实际读取字节、首次恢复延迟、稳态 ITL、预取浪费和容量峰值。正确性覆盖非整页前缀、共享分叉、位置偏移、量化元数据和页槽复用。\n\n长时间暂停和昂贵前缀有利于保存状态；短前缀、低带宽、繁忙存储或频繁读取可能更适合重算或改变计算放置。只有完整状态契约允许的方案才能参与性能比较。\n\n<!-- infraagents:provenance -->\n\n## 来源与推导\n\n- "references/S-DISTSERVE/fulltext.txt", "sha256": "3a5cab6f9b9ac40c7fd396c5da1a021a9a3df666f6945f88e58a4a6a393477fd", "reading_scope": "excerpt", "category": "source_original", "content": "[EXCERPT extracted-character range 12000:20000]\nrast, despite process-\ning only one new token per step, the decoding phase incurs a\nsimilar level of I/O to the prefill phase, making it constrained\nby the GPU’s memory bandwidth.\nDuring both phases, intermediate states, known as KV\ncaches [32], are generated at each token position, which are\nneeded again in later decoding steps. To avoid recomputing\nthem, they are saved in GPU memory. Because of the shared\nuse of LLM weights and KV caches in memory, most LLM in-\nference engines opt to colocate the prefill and decoding phases\non GPUs, despite their distinct computational characteristics.\n2.2\nLLM Serving Optimization\nIn real-time online serving, multiple requests come and must\nbe served within SLOs. Batching and parallelizing their com-\nputation is key for achieving low latency, high throughput,\nand high utilization of GPUs.\nBatching. Current serving systems [9,32,54] utilize a batch-\ning technique known as continuous batching. This method\nbatches the prefill of new requests with the decoding of on-\ngoing ones. It boosts the GPU utilization and maximizes\nthe overall system throughput – tokens generated per second\nacross all users and requests. However, as mentioned in §1\nand elaborated later in §2.3, this approach leads to trade-offs\nbetween TTFT and TPOT. An advanced variant of contin-\nuous batching [9] attempts to balance TTFT and TPOT by\nsegmenting long prefill into chunks and attaching decoding\njobs with a chunked prefill – but essentially, it trades TTFT\nfor TPOT and cannot eliminate the interference (§2.3). In\nsummary, batching prefill and decoding invariably leads to\ncompromises in either TTFT or TPOT.\nModel parallelism. In LLM serving, model parallelism is\ngenerally divided as intra- and inter-operator parallelisms [33,\n46,59]. Both can be used to support larger models but may\nimpact serving performance differently. Intra-operator paral-\nlelism partitions computationally intensive operators, such\nas matrix multiplications, across multiple GPUs, accelerat-\ning computation but causing substantial communication. It\nreduces the execution time3, hence latency, particularly for\nTTFT of the prefill phase, but requires high bandwidth con-\nnectivity between GPUs (e.g., NVLINK). Inter-operator par-\nallelism organizes LLM layers into stages, each running on\na GPU to form pipelines. It moderately increases execution\ntime due to inter-stage communication, but linearly scales the\nsystem’s rate capacity with each added GPU. In this paper,\nwe reveal an additional benefit of model parallelism: reduced\nqueuing delay of both prefill and decoding phases, steaming\nfrom shorter execution time. We delve into this further in\n§3. Besides model parallelism, replicating a model instance,\nirrespective of its model parallelism configurations, linearly\nscales the system’s rate capacity.\nThese parallelism strategies create a complex space of op-\ntimization that requires careful trade-offs based on the appli-\ncation’s latency requirements.\n2.3\nProblems and Opportunities\nColocating and batching the prefill and decoding computation\nto maximize the overall system throughput, as in existing\nsystems, is cost-effective for service providers. However, in\nthe presence of SLOs, present approaches struggle to main-\ntain both high service quality and low cost due to the issues\ndiscussed below.\nPrefill-decoding interference. As Figure 2 shows, adding a\nsingle prefill job to a batch of decoding requests significantly\nslows down both processes, leading to a marked increase in\nTTFT and TPOT. Specifically, the decoding tasks in the batch\nmust wait for lengthier prefill jobs to complete, thus extending\nTPOT; the slowdown intensifies with a longer prefill, shown\nin Figure 2(b). Adding decoding jobs to prefill also increases\nthe time to complete the prefill task, particularly when the\nGPU is already at capacity (Figure 2 blue curves).\nOne attempt to mitigate this interference is called chunked-\nprefill with piggyback [3,9]. It proposes to split the long prefill\n3we emphasize “execution time” instead of latency here because latency\ncomprises both execution time and queuing delay.\n3\n\n\n--- PAGE 4 ---\n0\n50\n100\n150\n200\n250\nBatch Size\n0\n5\n10\n15\n20\n25\n30\nLatency (ms)\ndecoding slowdown\nprefill slowdown\ndecoding-with-one-prefill\ndecoding-only\n0\n25\n50\n75\n100\n125\nBatch Size\n25\n50\n75\n100\n125\n150\nLatency (ms)\ndecoding slowdown\nprefill slowdown\n(a) Input length = 128\n(b) Input length = 1024\nFigure 2: Batch execution time when serving a 13B LLM\nas batch size increases. Compared between a decoding-only\nbatch and the batch adding one more prefill job.\ninto chunks and batch a prefill chunk with a few decoding jobs\n(a.k.a. piggybacking). This technique alleviates the slowdown\nof the decoding job caused by the long prefill job, but it does\nnot eliminate it. Additionally, it results in an extra overhead\nfor the prefill job which cannot be easily mitigated by adjust-\ning the chunk size. First, if the chunk size is set much lower\nthan the inflection point that can saturate the GPU, then the\nprefill job will have a longer execution time since it competes\nwith the decoding job in the same batch and cannot solely\nutilize the GPU resources. Second, if we increase the chunk\nsize to nearly saturate the GPU, the chance of piggybacking\nwill diminish since the remaining slots for decode tokens are\nlimited. Also, chunked-prefill causes significantly more mem-\nory access for the prefill jobs. This is because the KV cache\nof all previous chunks have to be loaded from HBM to SRAM\nrepeatedly to compute each subsequent chunk. Concretely,\nif a prefill job is split into N equal chunks, we need to load\nN +(N −1)+...+1 = O(N2) chunks of KV Cache in total,\ncompared to O(N) in the non-chunked case. This overhead\nwill increase as the context length becomes longer.\nIneffective scheduling. Unbatching prefill and decoding jobs\nand scheduling them sequentially does not mitigate the inter-\nference. Decoding jobs may experience longer queuing delays\ndue to waiting for ongoing prefill jobs on GPUs. Moreover,\nbatches dedicated to decoding often lead to GPU underutiliza-\ntion. Prioritizing tasks in either phase adversely affects the\nlatency of the other, rendering priority scheduling ineffective.\nResource and parallelism coupling. Colocating prefill and\ndecoding phases on the same GPUs unavoidably share their\nresource and parallelism settings. However, each phase has its\nunique computational characteristic and latency requirement\nthat calls for more heterogeneous resource allocation. For\nexample, the prefill phase tends to be compute-bound and\nbenefits from more intra-op parallelism to reduce execution\ntime to meet the tight SLO on TTFT. By contrast, the opti-\nmal parallelism configuration of the decoding phase depends\non the running batch size. In existing systems, due to cou-\npling, resource allocation and parallelism plans are tailored\nto satisfy the more demanding of TTFT and TPOT, which\nmay not be ideal for the other. This often leads to resource\nover-provisioning to meet both SLOs.\nOpportunities. To address these issues, we propose to dis-\naggregate the prefill and decoding phases. We use the term\ninstance to denote a unit of resources that manages exactly\none complete copy of model weights. One instance can cor-\nrespond to many GPUs when model parallelism is applied.\nNote that when we disaggregate the two phases to different\nGPUs, each phase manages its copy of the model weights,\nresulting in prefill instances and decoding instances. A prefill\ninstance, upon receiving a request, performs only the prefill\ncomputation for this request to generate the first output token.\nIt then sends the intermediate results (mainly KV caches)\nto a decoding instance, which is responsible for subsequent\ndecoding steps. Because decoding computation often has low\nGPU utilization, we may allocate multiple prefill instances\nper decoding instance. This allows batching more decoding\njobs to achieve higher GPU utilization.\nDisaggregating prefill and decoding natur\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 28000:52000]\n is\nnecessary to maintain the KV caches for all active requests.\nScaling the decoding instance with model parallelism or lever-\naging advanced memory management techniques for LLM\nKV caches, such as Paged-Attention [32] and GQA [10],\nenable further scaling of the decoding batch size to nearly\ncompute-bound. As the decoding batch size continue to in-\ncrease to approach the compute-bound, the decoding compu-\ntation begins to resemble the prefill phase. With this observa-\ntion, we investigate how the latency and throughput change\nunder different parallelism degrees under large batch condi-\ntions in Figure 5: intra-op parallelism reduces latency with\ndiminishing returns, caused by communication and reduced\nutilization after partitioning. Inter-op parallelism can almost\nlinearly scale the throughput. Hence, when the TPOT SLO\nis stringent, intra-op parallelism is essential to reduce TPOT\nto meet latency goals. Beyond this, inter-op parallelism is\npreferable to enhance throughput linearly.\nIt is worth noting that when the model can fit into the mem-\nory of a single GPU, replication is a competitive option in\naddition to model parallelism for both prefill and decoding\ninstances, to linearly scale the system’s rate capacity. It may\nalso reduce the queuing delay – as indicated by Eq. 1 – by\nsubstituting R with R/N assuming requests are equally dis-\npatched to N replicas, at the cost of maintaining additional\nreplicas of the model weights in GPU memory.\n3.3\nPractical Problems\nWe have developed foundational principles for selecting batch-\ning and parallelisms for each phase. In this section, we discuss\nand address several challenges encountered during the practi-\ncal deployment of disaggregated prefill and decoding phases.\nVariable prefill length. §3 has assumed uniform prompt\nlength across requests. In real deployments, depending on\nthe LLM application, the lengths of requests are non-uniform.\nThe non-uniformity can cause pipeline bubbles [28,36] for\nprefill instances applying inter-op parallelism because the\nexecution time of pipeline stages across requests of different\nlengths will vary. This results in slight deviations from the\nconclusions indicated by using the M/D/1 queue model. To\naddress the problem, §4 develops algorithms that search for\nparallelisms based on workloads, and resort to scheduling to\nminimize the bubbles (§4.3).\nCommunication overhead. Transferring KV caches from\nprefill to decoding instances incurs notable overheads. For\nexample, the KV cache size of a single 512-token request\non OPT-66B is approximately 1.13GB. Assuming an aver-\nage arrival rate of 10 rps, we need to transfer 11.3GB data\nper second—or equivalently 90Gbps bandwidth to render the\noverhead invisible. While many modern GPU clusters for\nLLMs are equipped with InfiniBand (e.g., 800 Gbps), in cases\nwhere cross-node bandwidth is limited, DistServe relies on\nthe commonly available intra-node NVLINK, where the peak\nbandwidth between A100 GPUs is 600 GB/s, again rendering\nthe transmission overhead negligible (see §6.3). However, this\nrequirement imposes additional constraints on the placement\nof prefill and decoding instances that we take into considera-\ntion in the next section.\nThrough the analysis in this section, we identify the work-\nload pattern, placement constraints, SLO requirements, paral-\nlelism strategies, and resource allocation as key parameters\nthat create a web of considerations in designing the disag-\ngregated serving system. How to automatically navigate the\nsearch space to find the configuration that achieves optimal\nper-gpu goodput is challenging, and addressed next.\n4\nMethod\nWe built DistServe to solve the above challenges. Given the\nmodel, workload characteristic, latency requirements, and\nSLO attainment target, DistServe will determine (a) the par-\nallelism strategies for prefill and decoding instances, (b) the\nnumber of each instance type to deploy, as well as (c) how\nto place them onto the physical cluster. We call the solution\na placement. Our goal is to find a placement that maximizes\nthe per-gpu goodput.\nAs explained in §3.3, a key design consideration is to man-\nage communications between disaggregated prefill and de-\ncoding phases, given varying cluster setups. In this section,\nwe first present two placement algorithms: one for clusters\nwith high-speed cross-node networks (§4.1) and the other\nfor environments lacking such infrastructure (§4.2); the lat-\nter introduces additional constraints. We then develop online\nscheduling optimizations that adapt to the nuances of real-\nworld workloads (§4.3).\n6\n\n\n--- PAGE 7 ---\nAlgorithm 1 High Node-Affinity Placement Algorithm\nInput: LLM G, #node limit per-instance N, #GPU per-node\nM, GPU memory capacity C, workload W, traffic rate R.\nOutput: the placement best_plm.\nconfigp,configd ←/0, /0\nfor intra_op ∈{1,2,...,M} do\nfor inter_op ∈{1,2,...,\nN×M\nintra_op} do\nif\nG.size\ninter_op×intra_op < C then\nconfig ←(inter_op,intra_op)\nˆG ←parallel(G,config)\nconfig.goodput ←simu_prefill( ˆG,W)\nif\nconfigp.goodput\ncon figp.num_gpus <\nconfig.goodput\ncon fig.num_gpus then\nconfigp ←config\nconfig.goodput ←simu_decode( ˆG,W)\nif\nconfigd.goodput\ncon figd.num_gpus <\nconfig.goodput\ncon fig.num_gpus then\nconfigd ←config\nn,m ←⌈\nR\nconfigp.goodput⌉,⌈\nR\nconfigd.goodput⌉\nbest_plm ←(n,configp,m,configd)\nreturn best_plm\n4.1\nPlacement for High Node-Affinity Cluster\nOn high node-affinity clusters equipped with Infiniband, KV\ncaches transmission overhead across nodes is negligible, Dist-\nServe can deploy prefill and decoding instances across any\ntwo nodes without constraints. We propose a two-level place-\nment algorithm for such scenarios: we first optimize the par-\nallelism configurations for prefill and decoding instances sep-\narately to attain phase-level optimal per-gpu goodput; then,\nwe use replication to match the overall traffic rate.\nHowever, finding the optimal parallel configuration for a\nsingle instance type, such as for the prefill instance, is still\nchallenging, due to the lack of a simple analytical formula to\ncalculate the SLO attainment (a.k.a., percentage of requests\nthat meet TTFT requirement), given that the workload has\ndiverse input, output lengths, and irregular arrival patterns.\nGauging the SLO via real-testbed profiling is time-prohibitive.\nWe thus resort to building a simulator to estimate the SLO at-\ntainment, assuming prior knowledge of the workload’s arrival\nprocess and input and output length distributions. Although\nshort-term interval is impossible to predict, the workload\npattern over longer timescales (e.g., hours or days) is often\npredictable [33, 55]. DistServe fits a distribution from the\nhistory request traces and resamples new traces from the dis-\ntribution as the input workload to the simulator to compute\nthe SLO attainment. Next, DistServe simply enumerates the\nplacements and finds the maximum rate that meets the SLO\nattainment target with binary search and simulation trials.\nAlgorithm 1 outlines the process. We enumerate all feasible\nparallel configurations, subject to cluster capacity limit, for\nboth prefill and decoding instances. Then, for a specific pre-\nfill phase configuration, we use simu_prefill to simulate\nAlgorithm 2 Low Node-Affinity Placement Algorithm\nInput: LLM G, #node limit per-instance N, #GPU per-node\nM, GPU memory capacity C, workload W, traffic rate R.\nOutput: the placement best_plm.\nconfig∗←/0\nfor inter_op ∈{1,2,...,N} do\nP ←get_intra_node_configs(G,M,C,inter_op)\nfor Pp ∈P do\nfor Pd ∈P do\nif Pp.num_gpus+Pd.num_gpus ≤M then\nconfig ←(inter_op,Pp,Pd)\nˆGp, ˆGd ←parallel(G,config)\nconfig.goodput ←simulate( ˆGp, ˆGd,W)\nif\nconfig.∗goodput\nconfig.∗num_gpus <\nconfig.goodput\nconfig.num_gpus then\nconfig∗←config\nn ←⌈\nR\nconfig.∗goodput⌉\nbest_plm ←(n,config∗)\nreturn best_plm\nand find its maximum goodput via binary search (similarly\nfor using simu_decode for decoding). After determining the\noptimal parallel configurations for both prefill and decoding\ninstances, we replicate them to achieve the user-required over-\nall traffic rate according to their goodput.\nThe complexity of Algorithm 1 is O(NM2), with N as the\nnode limit per instance and M representing the typical number\nof GPUs per node in modern clusters (e.g., 8). The search\nspace is manageable and the solving time is under 1.3 minutes\nin our largest setting, as demonstrated in §6.5.\nSimulator building. Algorithm 1 relies on a simulator to es-\ntimate the goodput under various SLOs and SLO attainment\ngoals given the workload and the parallelism plan. To build an\naccurate simulator, we analyze the FLOPs and the number of\nmemory accesses for prefill and decoding phases respectively,\nand use a latency model to approximate the inference execu-\ntion time. See details in Appendix A. The simulator aligns\nwell with real profiling results, thanks to the high predictabil-\nity of DNN workloads [23,33], verified in §6.4.\nBy far, we have developed Algorithm 1 assuming we can\nplace the prefill and decoding instance between any two nodes\n(or on the same node) of the cluster, and the KV cache trans-\nmission utilizes high bandwidth network. In many real clus-\nters, GPUs inside a node access to high-bandwidth NVLINK\nwhile GPUs distributed across nodes have limited bandwidth.\nWe next develop an algorithm to address this constraint.\n4.2\nPlacement for Low Node-Affinity Cluster\nA straightforward solution is to always colocate prefill and\ndecoding instances on the same node, utilizing the NVLINK,\nwhich is commonly available inside a GPU node. For large\nmodels, e.g. with 175B parameters (350GB), we may be un-\nable to even host a single pair of prefill and decoding instances\n7\n\n\n--- PAGE 8 ---\nin an 8-GPU node (80G × 8 = 640G < 350 × 2GB). We in-\ncorporate this as additional placement constraints and co-\noptimize it with model parallelism, presented in Algorithm 2.\nThe key insight is that KV cache transfer occurs exclu-\nsively between corresponding layers of prefill and decoding\ninstances. Leveraging inter-op parallelism, we group layers\ninto stages and divide each instance into segments, termed\nas instance segments, with each segment maintaining one\nspecific inter-op stage. By colocating prefill and decoding\nsegments of the same stage within a single node, we force\nthe transfer of intermediate states to occur only via NVLINK.\nInside a node, we set the same parallelism and resource allo-\ncation for segments of the same instance. Given the typical\nlimitation of GPUs per node (usually 8), we can enumerate\npossible configurations inside one node and use the simulator\nto identify the configurations that yield the best goodput.\nAs outlined in Algorithm 2, we begin by enumerating inter-\nop parallelism degrees to get all the possible instance seg-\nments. For each segment, we get all possible intra-node paral-\nlelism configurations by calling get_intra_node_configs.\nThen we use simulation to find the optimal one and replicate\nit to satisfy the target traffic rate.\n4.3\nOnline scheduling\nThe runtime architecture of DistServe is shown in Figure 6.\nDistServe operates with a simple FCFS scheduling policy.\nAll incoming requests arrive at a centralized controller, then\ndispatched to the prefill instance with the shortest queue for\nprefill processing, followed by dispatch to the least loaded de-\ncoding instance for decoding steps. This setup, while simple,\nis optimized with several key enhancements tailored to the\nnuances of real-world workloads.\nReducing pipeline bubbles. To mitigate the pipeline bubbles\ncaused by non-uniform prompt lengths (§3.3), we schedule\nthe requests in a way that balances the execution time across\nall batches in the pipeline. This is achieved by noting that,\nfor both prefill and decoding instances, the number of new\ntokens in the batch is a reliable indicator of the batch’s real\nexecution time. For prefill instances, we profile the target\nmodel and GPU to figure out the shortest prompt length Lm\nneeded to saturate the GPU. We schedule prefill batches with a\ntotal sequence length close to Lm, by either batching multiple\nrequests shorter than Lm or individually scheduling requests\nlonger than Lm. For decoding instances, we set Lm as the\nlargest batch size.\nCombat busrtiness. Burstiness in workloads can cause a\ndeluge of KV caches to transfer from prefill to decoding in-\nstances, risking memory overload on decoding instances. To\ncircumvent this, DistServe employs a “pull” method for KV\ncache transmission rather than a “push” approach – decoding\ninstances fetch KV cache from prefill instances as needed, us-\ning the GPU memory of prefill instances as a queuing buffer.\nThis way, the prefill instance can continue handling other\nController\nPrefill Instance\nRequests\nParallel Runtime\nGPU\nGPU\nLLM Model\nGPU\nGPU\nParallel Runtime\nDecoding Instance\nGPU\nGPU\nLLM Model\nGPU\nGPU\nKV Cache \nTransfer\nParallel Runtime\nFigure 6: DistServe Runtime System Architecture\nprefill jobs by simply retaining the KV Cache in the GPU\nmemory after processing the prompt. Hence, each type of in-\nstance operates at its own pace without complex coordination.\nReplaning. The resource and parallelism plan in DistServe is\noptimized for a specific workload pattern, which may become\nsuboptimal if the workload pattern changes over time. Dist-\nServe implement periodic replanning. A workload profiler\nmonitors key parameters such as the average input and output\nlength of the requests, the average arrival rate, etc. If a signif-\nicant pattern shift is detected, DistServe will trigger a rerun\nof the placement algorithm based on recent historical data.\nThis process is expedient – the proposed algorithm runs in\nseconds (§6.5) and reloading LLM weights can be completed\nwithin minutes – far shorter than the hourly scale at which\nreal-world workload variations tend to occur.\nPreemption and fault tolerance. DistServe does not imple-\nment advanced runtime policies like preemption [26] and\nfault tolerance [58], which are complementary to disaggre-\ngation. Nevertheless, we discuss how they fit into DistServe.\nIn DistServe, the FCFS policy can lead to a “convoy effect”,\nwhere longer requests block shorter ones in the prefill stage.\nIncorporating preemptive strategies, as suggested in existing\nliterature [53], could enhance efficiency and is feasible within\nour system’s architecture. While not a primary focus in the\ncurrent DistServe, fault tolerance is a critical aspect for con-\nsideration. In traditional colocation- and replication-based\nsystems, a fault in one instance typically does not disrupt\nother replica instances. However, in DistServe, the depen-\ndency between prefill and decoding instances introduces the\nrisk of fault propagation. For example, a fault in a single de-\ncoding instance mapped to multiple prefill instances could\npotentially cripple the entire service and cluster. We leave\nboth as future work.\n5\nImplementation\nDistServe is an end-to-end distributed serving system for\nLLMs with a placement algorithm module, a RESTful API\nfrontend, an orchestration layer, and a parallel execution en-\ngine. The algorithm module, frontend, and orchestration layer\nare implemented with 6.5K lines of Python code. The par-\nallel execution engine is implemented with 8.1K lines of\n8\n\n\n--- PAGE 9 ---\nApplication\nModel Size\nTTFT\nTPOT\nDataset\nChatbot OPT-13B\n26GB\n0.25s\n0.1s\nShareGPT [8]\nChatbot OPT-66B\n132GB\n2.5s\n0.15s\nShareGPT [8]\nChatbot OPT-175B\n350GB\n4.0s\n0.2s\nShareGPT [8]\nCode Completion OPT-66B\n132GB\n0.125s\n0.2s\nHumanEval [14]\nSummarization OPT-66B\n132GB\n15s\n0.15s\nLongBench [13]\nTable 1: Workloads in evaluation and latency requirements.\n0\n500\n1000\n1500\n2000\n# Tokens\n0\n2\n4\n6\n8\nDensity\n1e\n3\nInput (avg=755.5)\nOutput (avg=200.3)\n0\n500\n1000\n1500\n2000\n# Tokens\n0\n2\n4\n6\n1e\n3\nInput (avg=171.3)\nOutput (avg=98.2)\n0\n500\n1000\n1500\n2000\n# Tokens\n0\n5\n10\n15\n1e\n3\nInput (avg=1738.3)\nOutput (avg=90.7)\n(a) ShareGPT\n(b) HumanEval\n(c) LongBench\nFigure 7: The input and output length distributions of (a)\nShareGPT, (b) HumanEval, and (c) LongBench datasets.\nC++/CUDA code.\nThe placement algorithm module implements the algorithm\nand the simulator mentioned in §4 which gives the placement\ndecision for a specific model and cluster setting. The fron-\ntend supports an OpenAI API-compatible interface where\nclients can specify the sampling parameters like maximum\noutput length and temperature. The orchestration layer man-\nages the prefill and decoding instances, responsible for request\ndispatching, KV cache transmission, and results delivery. It\nutilizes NCCL [6] for cross-node GPU communication and\nasynchronous CudaMemcpy for intra-node communication,\nwhich avoids blocking the GPU computation during transmis-\nsion. Each instance is powered by a parallel execution engine,\nwhich uses Ray [35] actor to implement GPU workers that\nexecute the LLM inference and manage the KV Cache in\na distributed manner. It integrates many recent LLM opti-\nmizations like continuous batching [54], FlashAttention [20],\nPagedAttention [32] and supports popular open-source LLMs\nsuch as OPT [56] and LLaMA [51].\n6\nEvaluation\nIn this section, we evaluate DistServe under different sizes\nof LLMs ranging from 13B to 175B and various applica-\ntion datasets including chatbot, code-completion, and sum-\nmarization. The evaluation shows that DistServe consistently\noutperforms the current state-of-the-art system across all the\nsettings (§6.2). Specifically, DistServe can handle up to 7.4×\nhigher rates and 12.6× more stringent SLO while meeting\nthe latency requirements for over 90% requests. Addition-\nally, we analyze the latency breakdown in DistServe to show\nthe communication overhead is insubstantial thanks to our\nbandwidth-aware placement algorithm (§6.3) and do abla-\ntion studies of our techniques (§6.4). Finally, we profile the\nexecution time of our placement algorithm (§6.5).\n6.1\nExperiments Setup\nCluster testbed. We deploy DistServe on a cluster with 4\nnodes and 32 GPUs. Each node has 8 NVIDIA SXM A100-\n80GB GPUs connected with NVLINK. The cross-node band-\nwidth is 25Gbps. Due to the limited cross-node bandwidth,\nwe use the low node-affinity placement algorithm (§2) for\nDistServe in most of the experiments except for the ablation\nstudy (§6.4) which uses simulation.\nModel and workloads setup. Similar to prior work on LLM\nserving [32], we choose the OPT [56] model series, which\nis a representative LLM family widely used in academia and\nindustry. Newer GPT model families are adopting memory-\nefficient attention mechanisms like GQA [10] and MQA [44].\nDistServe will show better performance on these models be-\ncause the transmission overhead is lower due to the decrease\nin KV cache size. We choose OPT which uses the classic\nMHA [52] to put enough pressure on the transmission over-\nhead. We use FP16 precision in all experiments. For work-\nloads, as shown in Table 1, We choose three typical LLM\napplications and set the SLOs empirically based on their ser-\nvice target because there exists no available SLO settings for\nthese applications as far as we know. For each application,\nwe select a suitable dataset and sample requests from it for\nevaluation. Since all the datasets do not include timestamps,\nwe generate request arrival times using Poisson distribution\nwith different request rates. Due to the space limit, we test the\nchatbot workload on all three OPT models and the other two\nworkloads on OPT-66B, which matches the largest size in the\nrecent open-source LLM series [51].\n• Chatbot [1]: We use the ShareGPT dataset [8] for the\nchatbot application, which is a collection of user-shared\nconversations with ChatGPT. For OPT-13B, the TTFT SLO\nis set to 0.25s for responsiveness and the TPOT SLO is\nset to 0.1s which is higher than the normal human read\nspeed. For OPT-66B and OPT-175B, we slightly relax the\ntwo SLOs due to the increase in model execution latency.\n• Code completion [14]: We use the HumanEval [14] dataset\nfor the code completion task. It includes 164 programming\nproblems with a function signature or docstring which is\nused to evaluate the performance of code completion mod-\nels. Since the code completion model is used as a personal\nreal-time coding assistant, we set both SLOs to be stringent.\n• Summarization [5]: It is a popular LLM task to generate\na concise summary for a long article, essay, or even an\nacademic paper. We use LongBench [13] dataset which\ncontains the summarization task4. As shown in Figure 7,\nLongBench has much longer input lengths than the other\ntwo datasets. So we set a loose TTFT SLO but require a\nstringent TPOT.\nMetrics. We use SLO attainment as the major evaluation met-\nric. Under a specific SLO attainment goal (say, 90%), we are\n4We capped the input lengths in LongBench because OPT’s absolute\npositional embedding only supports a maximum length of 2048.\n9\n\n\n--- PAGE 10 ---\n1\n2\n3\nPer-GPU Rate (req/s)\n0\n50\n100\nSLO Attainment (%)\n0.25\n0.50\n0.75\n1.00\nPer-GPU Rate (req/s)\n0\n50\n100\n0.05\n0.10\n0.15\n0.20\n0.25\nPer-GPU Rate (req/s)\n0\n50\n100\nDistServe\nDeepSpeed-MII\nvLLM\n0.75\n1.00\n1.25\n1.50\nSLO Scale\n0\n50\n100\nSLO Attainment (%) \n3.0\n2.5\n2.0\n1.5\n1.0\nSLO Scale\n0\n50\n100\n0.75\n1.00\n1.25\n1.50\nSLO Scale\n0\n50\n100\n(a) OPT-13B\n(b) OPT-66B\n(C) OPT-175B\nFigure 8: Chatbot application with OPT models on the ShareGPT dataset.\nconcerned with two things: the maximum per-GPU goodput\nand the minimal SLO the system can handle. We are partic-\nularly interested in an SLO attainment of 90% (indicated by\nthe vertical lines in all curve plots), but will also vary the\nrate and latency requirements to observe how the SLO attain-\nment changes. We also include the results in the Appendix for\nan SLO attainment of 99% to show the system performance\nunder a more stringent SLO attainment target.\nBaselines. We compare DistServe to two baseline systems:\n• vLLM [32]: vLLM is a representative LLM serving sys-\ntem widely used in both academia and industry. It sup-\nports continuous batching [54] to increase throughput and\npaged-attention [32] to reduce memory fragmentation dur-\ning KV cache allocation. However, it colocates the prefill\nand decoding computation to maximize the overall system\nthroughput and struggles to meet the latency requirements\ncost-efficiently. Since vLLM only supports intra-op paral-\nlelism, we follow previous work [32] to set intra-op equals\n1, 4, and 8 for the three OPT models, respectively.\n• DeepSpeed-MII [3]: DeepSpeed Model Implementations\nfor Inference (MII) supports chunked-prefill by decompos-\ning long prompts into smaller chunks and composing with\nshort prompts to exactly fill a target token budget. It miti-\ngates but cannot eliminate the prefill-decoding interference\ncaused by the long prefill job. We set its intra-op the same\nas vLLM for OPT-13B and OPT-66B for a fair comparison.\nHowever, DeepSpeed-MII cannot serve OPT-175B whose\nvocab_size = 50272 because its underlying kernel imple-\nmentation requires vocab_size/intra_op is a multiple of\n8 where intra-op equals 8 does not satisfy. Setting intra-\nop equals 4 can satisfy this requirement but will cause the\nout-of-memory issue.\n6.2\nEnd-to-end Experiments\nIn this Section, we compare the end-to-end performance of\nDistServe against the baselines on real application datasets.\nChatbot. We evaluate the performance of DistServe on the\nchatbot application for all three OPT models. The first row\nof Figure 8 illustrates that when we gradually increase the\nrate, more requests will violate the latency requirements and\nthe SLO attainment decreases. The vertical line shows the\nmaximum per-GPU rate the system can handle to meet latency\nrequirements for over 90% of the requests.\nOn the ShareGPT dataset, DistServe can sustain 2.0×–\n4.6× higher request rate compared to vLLM. This is because\nDistLLM eliminates the prefill-decoding interference through\ndisaggregation. Two phases can optimize their own objec-\ntives by allocating different resources and employing tailored\nparallelism strategies. Specifically, by analyzing the chosen\nplacement strategy5 for 175B, we find the prefill instance\nhas inter-op = 3, intra-op = 3; and the decoding instan\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 60000:70667]\nailored parallelism strategies for prefill\nand decoding instances and optimize their targets without the\ncoupling effects.\n6.5\nAlgorithm Running Time\nFigure 12 shows the running time for Alg. 1 (DistServe-Low)\nand Alg. 2 (DistServe-High) on an AWS m5d.metal instance\nwith 96 cores as the number of GPUs (N ×M) provided to a\nsingle instance increases. According to the results, DistServe\nscales well with the number of GPUs and is independent of\nthe model size. This is because the simulator only simulates\ndiscrete events and the running time is the same no matter\nhow big the model is. On the other hand, both algorithms are\nhighly parallelizable, as the searches for different parallelism\nstrategies are independent of each other, allowing the execu-\ntion time of the algorithms to accelerate almost linearly with\nmore CPU cores.\nAs the number of GPUs increases, the execution time of\n\"Dist-Low\" becomes higher than that of \"Dist-High\". This is\nbecause the search for parallelism strategies for prefill and\ndecoding instances in \"Dist-High\" is independent and can\nbe parallelized. But for \"Dist-Low\", due to additional restric-\ntions on deployment, we need to enumerate all the possible\nintra-node parallelism combinations for prefill and decoding\ninstances. Even so, the execution time of the algorithm is in\nminutes, and since it only needs to be executed once before\neach redeployment, this overhead is acceptable.\n7\nDiscussion\nIn this paper, we focus on the goodput-optimized setting and\npropose DistServe under the large-scale LLM serving sce-\nnario. As LLMs are widely used and deployed across vari-\nous service scenarios with different optimization targets and\nresource limits, it becomes almost impossible to find a one-\nsize-fits-all solution that effectively addresses all aspects of\nLLM serving. In this section, we discuss the pros and cons of\nDistServe and potentially better solutions in other scenarios.\nThroughput-optimized scenarios. In offline applications\nthat are not latency-sensitive, users typically have lower re-\nquirements for response time [45]. This allows serving sys-\ntems to shift focus towards maximizing overall throughput\ninstead of goodput and the effectiveness of DistServe may be\n12\n\n\n--- PAGE 13 ---\ncompromised. In this case, techniques such as chunked-prefill\nwith piggyback [3,9] may be preferred since it can fill each\nbatch to the compute-bound threshold, thereby maintaining\nhigher GPU utilization in every iteration.\nResource-constrained scenarios. Small-scale enterprises\nand individual researchers often lack the resources to deploy\nLLMs on large-scale clusters [45,48]. In resource-constrained\nscenarios, such as environments with only a few or even a\nsingle GPU, the design space for DistServe is significantly\nlimited. It struggles or even fails to adjust the parallel strate-\ngies and resource allocation to effectively enhance serving\nperformance. In this case, simpler architectural choices like\nnon-disaggregated systems [3, 32] may reduce deployment\ncomplexity and optimize operational efficiency.\nLong-context scenarios. Nowadays, more and more GPT\nmodels support extremely long contexts, such as Claude-\n3 [11], Gemini-1.5 [22], and Large World Model (LWM) [34],\nwhich all have a 1M context window. In such scenarios, the\ntransmission overhead will increase as the size of the KV\ncache grows linearly with the prompt length. However, the\nprefill computation grows quadratically, so the relative dura-\ntion of transmission and prefill job decreases. Meanwhile, a\nlonger context further exacerbates the disparity in computa-\ntional demands between prefill and decoding jobs, leading to\nincreased interference between them. Therefore, the disaggre-\ngation approach proposed in DistServe remains promising in\nlong-context serving.\n8\nRelated Work\nInference serving. There has been plenty of work on in-\nference serving recently. They range from general-purpose\nproduction-grade systems like TorchServe [7] and NVIDIA\nTriton [19] to systems optimized specifically for Transformer-\nbased LLMs [9, 18, 21, 33, 50, 53, 54, 60]. Among them,\nOrca [54] introduces continuous batching to increase through-\nput. vLLM [32] proposes paged-attention for fine-grained\nKV cache management. SARATHI [9] suggests a chunked-\nprefill approach, splitting a prefill request into chunks and\npiggybacking decoding requests to improve hardware utiliza-\ntion. FastServe [53] implements iteration-level preemptive\nscheduling to mitigate the queuing delay caused by long jobs.\nHowever, they all employ a colocation approach for prefill\nand decoding processing, thus leading to severe interference.\nThere are also concurrent works such as Splitwise [38], Tetri-\nInfer [27] and DéjàVu [49] which adopt similar disaggregation\nidea to optimize LLM inference, further confirming the ef-\nfectiveness of this method. Differently, DistServe emphasizes\nthe goodput optimization scenario more and takes a closer\nlook at the aspect of network bandwidth.\nGoodput-optimized systems. Optimizing goodput is a hot\ntopic in DL applications. Pollux [39] improves scheduling\nperformance in DL clusters by dynamically adjusting re-\nsources for jobs to increase cluster-wide goodput. Sia [29]\nintroduces a heterogeneous-aware scheduling approach that\ncan efficiently match cluster resources to elastic resource-\nadaptive jobs. Clockwork [23] and Shepherd [55] provide\nlatency-aware scheduling and preemption to improve the serv-\ning goodput, but they only target traditional small models.\nAlpaServe [33] focuses on LLMs, employing model paral-\nlelism to statistically multiplex the GPU execution thus im-\nproving the resource utilization. However, it only targets the\nnon-autoregressive generation. DistServe is the first work to\noptimize the goodput for autoregressive LLM inference.\nResource disaggregation. Resource disaggregated sys-\ntems [17,25,43] decouple the hardware resources from the\ntraditional monolithic server infrastructure and separate them\ninto resource pools to manage independently. It allows for\nmore flexible, efficient, and scalable deployment and increases\nresource utilization. Many applications benefit from a truly\ndisaggregated data center with high-speed network bandwidth\nand heterogenous hardware support [12,30,57]. DistServe\nshares the concept by disaggregating its system components,\nallowing for independent resource scaling and management.\nModel parallelism for training. DistServe is orthogonal\nto the large body of work on model parallelism in train-\ning [28,36,40,46,59]. As described in §3.3, inference-serving\nworkloads have unique characteristics not found in training\nsettings. Where these systems do intersect with DistServe, is\nin their methods for implementing model parallelism along\nvarious dimensions. DistServe can integrate new parallelism\noptimizations into its placement searching algorithm.\n9\nConclusion\nWe present DistServe, a new LLM serving architecture that\ndisaggregates the prefill and decoding computation. DistServe\nmaximizes the per-gpu goodput – the maximum request rate\nthat can be served adhering to the SLO attainment goal for\neach GPU provisioned, hence resulting in up to 7.4× lower\ncost per LLM query with guaranteed satisfaction of SLOs.\nOur findings affirm that as latency becomes an increasingly\nimportant metric for LLM services, prefill and decoding dis-\naggregation is a vital strategy in promising improved perfor-\nmance and service quality guarantees.\nAcknowledgments. We sincerely thank our shepherd and the\nanonymous reviewers for their valuable feedback. Thus\nthe GEMMs dominate the overall latency and our analysis\nprimarily focuses on them.\nA.1\nSymbol Definition\nHere are symbols related to the architecture of the model:\n• h: hidden size\n• n: number of heads\n• s: head size (h = n·s)\n• m: FFN intermediate size\nNote: If tensor parallelism is used, h, n, and m should be\ndivided by the tensor parallelism size.\nBelow are symbols that characterize the batch to be exe-\ncuted:\n• B: batch size\n• l0,l1,...,lB−1: input length of each request within the\nbatch\n• t: number of tokens in the batch, (t = ∑B−1\ni=0 li)\n• t2: squared sum of the input lengths (t2 = ∑B−1\ni=0 l2\ni )\n• b: block size in the attention kernel. This parameter is\nused in FlashAttention [20], a common kernel optimiza-\ntion technique adopted by current LLM serving systems.\nA.2\nPrefill Phase Latency Modeling\nSince the attention operation uses specially optimized kernels,\nwe first discuss the other four matrix multiplications in the\nprefill phase:\nGEMM Name\nShape of M\nShape of N\nQKV Linear\n(t,h)\n(h,3h)\nAttn Output\n(t,h)\n(h,h)\nFFN Input\n(t,h)\n(h,m)\nFFN Output\n(t,m)\n(m,h)\nThe arithmetic intensity (AI) of these operations is O(t).\nOn NVIDIA A100-80GB GPU, it is compute-bound when AI\nis over 156. Since t usually can reach several hundred in real\ncases, all of these operations are compute-bound. Therefore,\nwe can model the latency of these operations according to the\ntotal FLOPs:\nT1 = C1 ·(4th2 +2thm)\nNext, we discuss the prefill attention operation with\nFlashAttention [20] optimization. Since the attention only\noperates among the tokens in the same request, current im-\nplementations launch attention kernels for each request in\nthe same batch. For one attention head and a request with\nl tokens, the attention kernel needs to perform a total of\n2sl +3sl ·(l/b) ≈3sl ·(l/b) memory reads and writes, along-\nside 2sl2+sl(l/b) ≈2sl2 FLOPs. So the AI is 2b/3 = 10.677\n(when b = 16) or 21.333 (when b = 32), indicating that it is\na memory-bound operation on A100 GPU. Therefore, the\nwhole attention layer latency (including all requests and all\nheads) can be modeled as:\nT2 = C2 ·n·\nB−1\n∑\ni=0\n3sl2\ni\nb\n= C2 · 3nst2\nb\n= C2 · 3ht2\nb\nOverall, the latency of the prefill phase can be modeled as:\nTPrefill = C1 ·(4th2 +2thm)+C2 · 3ht2\nb\n+C3\nWe use C3 to quantify other overheads like Python Run-\ntime, system noise, and so on. Then we use profiling and\ninterpolation to figure out the values of C1, C2, and C3.\nA.3\nDecoding Phase Latency Modeling\nSimilarly, we first focus on the following GEMMs in the\ndecoding phase:\nGEMM Name\nShape of M\nShape of N\nQKV Linear\n(B,h)\n(h,3h)\nAttn Output\n(B,h)\n(h,h)\nFFN Input\n(B,h)\n(h,m)\nFFN Output\n(B,m)\n(m,h)\nThe AI of these operations is O(B). B is limited by the GPU\nmemory size and stringent latency requirements, so in existing\nserving scenarios, these operations are memory-bound. The\ntotal memory reads and writes is 8Bh + 4h2 + 2hm + 2Bm,\nand since h and m are usually significantly larger than B, we\ncan model the latency as:\nT3 = C4 ·(4h2 +2hm)\nAs for the decoding attention operation, for one attention\nhead and a request with l generated tokens, it needs to per-\nform 3sl memory reads and writes, alongside 2sl FLOPs. It\nis memory-bound, so we can model the latency of decoding\nattention as:\nT4 = C5 ·n·3s\nB−1\n∑\ni=0\nli = C5 ·3ht\nSumming up, the latency of the decoding phase is:\nTDecoding = C4 ·(4h2 +2hm)+C5 ·3ht\nHere we do not introduce the overhead term (like C3 in the\nprofiling stage) because 4h2 +2hm is already a constant, and\nthe overhead can be put into C4. Similarly, we use profiling\nand interpolation to figure out the values of C4 and C5.\n17\n\n\n--- PAGE 18 ---\n1\n2\n3\nPer-GPU Rate (req/s)\n0\n50\n100\nSLO Attainment (%)\n0.25\n0.50\n0.75\n1.00\nPer", "excerpt_ranges": [[12000, 20000], [28000, 52000], [60000, 70667], [72000, 76000], [80000, 84000]], "range_unit": "zero-based half-open characters in extracted text", "extracted_characters": 85689, "selected_characters": 50667, "extraction": "UTF-8 text", "selection_reason": "Ranked 4000-character windows by current entry title/tags/questions and algorithm/mechanism keywords; ties retain source order."}, {"path": "references/S-CUDA-TOPOLOGY/fulltext.txt", "sha256": "1f45872f110694d59f7e9b55ea37150648376f92ba85b0c3922b712a73736a32", "reading_scope": "excerpt", "category": "source_original", "content": "[EXCERPT extracted-character range 40000:42667]\nate memory. Kernels operate out of device memory, so the runtime provides functions to allocate, deallocate, and copy device memory, as well as transfer data between host memory and device memory.\nDevice memory can be allocated either as\nlinear memory\nor as\nCUDA arrays\n.\nCUDA arrays are opaque memory layouts optimized for texture fetching. They are described in\nTexture and Surface Memory\n.\nLinear memory is allocated in a single unified address space, which means that separately allocated entities can reference one another via pointers, for example, in a binary tree or linked list. The size of the address space depends on the host system (CPU) and the compute capability of the used GPU:\nTable 1\nLinear Memory Address Space\n\nx86_64 (AMD64)\nPOWER (ppc64le)\nARM64\nup to compute capability 5.3 (Maxwell)\n40bit\n40bit\n40bit\ncompute capability 6.0 (Pascal) or newer\nup to 47bit\nup to 49bit\nup to 48bit\nNote\nOn devices of compute capability 5.3 (Maxwell) and earlier, the CUDA driver creates an uncommitted 40bit virtual address reservation to ensure that memory allocations (pointers) fall into the supported range. This reservation appears as reserved virtual memory, but does not occupy any physical memory until the program actually allocates memory.\nLinear memory is typically allocated using\ncudaMalloc()\nand freed using\ncudaFree()\nand data transfer between host memory and device memory are typically done using\ncudaMemcpy()\n. In the vector addition code sample of\nKernels\n, the vectors need to be copied from host memory to device memory:\n// Device code\n__global__\nvoid\nVecAdd\n(\nfloat\n*\nA\n,\nfloat\n*\nB\n,\nfloat\n*\nC\n,\nint\nN\n)\n{\nint\ni\n=\nblockDim\n.\nx\n*\nblockIdx\n.\nx\n+\nthreadIdx\n.\nx\n;\nif\n(\ni\n<\nN\n)\nC\n[\ni\n]\n=\nA\n[\ni\n]\n+\nB\n[\ni\n];\n}\n// Host code\nint\nmain\n()\n{\nint\nN\n=\n...;\nsize_t\nsize\n=\nN\n*\nsizeof\n(\nfloat\n);\n// Allocate input vectors h_A and h_B in host memory\nfloat\n*\nh_A\n=\n(\nfloat\n*\n)\nmalloc\n(\nsize\n);\nfloat\n*\nh_B\n=\n(\nfloat\n*\n)\nmalloc\n(\nsize\n);\nfloat\n*\nh_C\n=\n(\nfloat\n*\n)\nmalloc\n(\nsize\n);\n// Initialize input vectors\n...\n// Allocate vectors in device memory\nfloat\n*\nd_A\n;\ncudaMalloc\n(\n&\nd_A\n,\nsize\n);\nfloat\n*\nd_B\n;\ncudaMalloc\n(\n&\nd_B\n,\nsize\n);\nfloat\n*\nd_C\n;\ncudaMalloc\n(\n&\nd_C\n,\nsize\n);\n// Copy vectors from host memory to device memory\ncudaMemcpy\n(\nd_A\n,\nh_A\n,\nsize\n,\ncudaMemcpyHostToDevice\n);\ncudaMemcpy\n(\nd_B\n,\nh_B\n,\nsize\n,\ncudaMemcpyHostToDevice\n);\n// Invoke kernel\nint\nthreadsPerBlock\n=\n256\n;\nint\nblocksPerGrid\n=\n(\nN\n+\nthreadsPerBlock\n-\n1\n)\n/\nthreadsPerBlock\n;\nVecAdd\n<<<\nblocksPerGrid\n,\nthreadsPerBlock\n>>>\n(\nd_A\n,\nd_B\n,\nd_C\n,\nN\n);\n// Copy result from device memory to host memory\n// h_C contains the result in host memory\ncudaMemcpy\n(\nh_C\n,\nd_C\n,\nsize\n,\ncudaM\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 76000:80000]\nmemory that is returned by\ncudaHostAlloc()\nor\nmalloc()\n, and one in device memory that can be retrieved using\ncudaHostGetDevicePointer()\nand then used to access the block from within a kernel. The only exception is for pointers allocated with\ncudaHostAlloc()\nand when a unified address space is used for the host and the device as mentioned in\nUnified Virtual Address Space\n.\nAccessing host memory directly from within a kernel does not provide the same bandwidth as device memory, but does have some advantages:\nThere is no need to allocate a block in device memory and copy data between this block and the block in host memory; data transfers are implicitly performed as needed by the kernel;\nThere is no need to use streams (see\nConcurrent Data Transfers\n) to overlap data transfers with kernel execution; the kernel-originated data transfers automatically overlap with kernel execution.\nSince mapped page-locked memory is shared between host and device however, the application must synchronize memory accesses using streams or events (see\nAsynchronous Concurrent Execution\n) to avoid any potential read-after-write, write-after-read, or write-after-write hazards.\nTo be able to retrieve the device pointer to any mapped page-locked memory, page-locked memory mapping must be enabled by calling\ncudaSetDeviceFlags()\nwith the\ncudaDeviceMapHost\nflag before any other CUDA call is performed. Otherwise,\ncudaHostGetDevicePointer()\nwill return an error.\ncudaHostGetDevicePointer()\nalso returns an error if the device does not support mapped page-locked host memory. Applications may query this capability by checking the\ncanMapHostMemory\ndevice property (see\nDevice Enumeration\n), which is equal to 1 for devices that support mapped page-locked host memory.\nNote that atomic functions (see\nAtomic Functions\n) operating on mapped page-locked memory are not atomic from the point of view of the host or other devices.\nAlso note that CUDA runtime requires that 1-byte, 2-byte, 4-byte, 8-byte, and 16-byte naturally aligned\n\nloads and stores to host memory initiated from the device are preserved as single accesses\n\nfrom the point of view of the host and other devices.\n\nOn some platforms, atomics to memory may be broken by the hardware into separate\n\nload and store operations.\n\nThese component load and store operations have the same requirements on preservation of naturally aligned accesses.\n\nThe CUDA runtime does not support a PCI Express bus topology where a PCI Express bridge splits\n\n8-byte naturally aligned operations and NVIDIA is not aware of any topology that splits\n\n16-byte naturally aligned operations.\n3.2.7.\nMemory Synchronization Domains\n\n3.2.7.1.\nMemory Fence Interference\n\nSome CUDA applications may see degraded performance due to memory fence/flush operations waiting on more transactions than those necessitated by the CUDA memory consistency model.\n__managed__\nint\nx\n=\n0\n;\n__device__\ncuda\n::\natomic\n<\nint\n,\ncuda\n::\nthread_scope_device\n>\na\n(\n0\n);\n__managed__\ncuda\n::\natomic\n<\nint\n,\ncuda\n::\nthread_scope_system\n>\nb\n(\n0\n);\nThread 1 (SM)\nx\n=\n1\n;\na\n=\n1\n;\nThread 2 (SM)\nwhile\n(\na\n!=\n1\n)\n;\nassert\n(\nx\n==\n1\n);\nb\n=\n1\n;\nThread 3 (CPU)\nwhile\n(\nb\n!=\n1\n)\n;\nassert\n(\nx\n==\n1\n);\nConsider the example above. Domain-related attributes set on the stream a graph is launched into are not used in execution of the graph.\n3.2.8.\nAsynchronous Concurrent Execution\n\nCUDA exposes the following operations as independent tasks that can operate concurrently with one another:\nComputation on the host;\nComputation on the device;\nMemory transfers from the host to the device;\nMemory transfers from the device to the host;\nMemory transfers within the memory of a given device;\nMemory transfers among devices.\nThe level of concurrency achieved between these operations will depend on the feature set and compute capability of the device as described below.\n3.2.8.1.\nConcurrent Execution between Host and Device\n\nConcurrent host execution is facilitated through asynchronous library functions that return control to the host thread before the device completes the requested task. Using asynchronous calls, many device operations can be queued up together to be executed by the CUDA driver when appropriate device resources are available. This relieves the host thread of much of the responsibility to manage the device, leaving it free for other tasks. The following device operations are asynchronous with respect to the host:\nKernel launches;\nMemory copies within a single device’s memory;\nMemory copies from host to device of a memory block of 64 KB or less;\nMemory copies performed by functions that are suffixed with\nAsync\n;\nMemory set function calls.\nProgrammers can globally disable asynchronicity of kernel launches for all CUDA applications running on a system by setting the\nCUDA_LAUNCH_BLOCKING\nenvironment variable to 1. This feature is provided for debugging purposes only and should not be used as a way to make production software run reliably.\nKernel launches are synchronous if hardware counters are collected via a profiler (Nsight, Visual Profiler) unless concurrent kernel profiling is enabled.\nAsync\nmemory copies might also be synchronous if they involve host memory that is not page-locked.\n3.2.8.2.\nConcurrent Kernel Execution\n\nSome devices of compute capability 2.x and higher can execute multiple kernels concurrently. Applications may query this capability by checking the\nconcurrentKernels\ndevice property (see\nDevice Enumeration\n), which is equal to 1 for devices that support it.\nThe maximum number of kernel launches that a device can execute concurrently depends on its compute capability and is listed in\nTable \n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 224000:228000]\nnce OpenGL can also consume memory and synchronization objects created in Vulkan, there exists an alternative approach to doing OpenGL-CUDA interop. Essentially, memory and synchronization objects exported by Vulkan could be imported into both, OpenGL and CUDA, and then used to coordinate memory accesses between OpenGL and CUDA. Please refer to the following OpenGL extensions for further details on how to import memory and synchronization objects exported by Vulkan:\nGL_EXT_memory_object\nGL_EXT_memory_object_fd\nGL_EXT_memory_object_win32\nGL_EXT_semaphore\nGL_EXT_semaphore_fd\nGL_EXT_semaphore_win32\n3.2.16.3.\nDirect3D 12 Interoperability\n\n3.2.16.3.1.\nMatching Device LUIDs\n\nWhen importing memory and synchronization objects exported by Direct3D 12, they must be imported and mapped on the same device as they were created on. The Nsight Compute version of the occupancy calculator is particularly useful as a learning tool that visualizes the impact of changes to the parameters that affect occupancy (block size, registers per thread, and shared memory per thread).\n5.3.\nMaximize Memory Throughput\n\nThe first step in maximizing overall memory throughput for the application is to minimize data transfers with low bandwidth.\nThat means minimizing data transfers between the host and the device, as detailed in\nData Transfer between Host and Device\n, since these have much lower bandwidth than data transfers between global memory and the device.\nThat also means minimizing data transfers between global memory and the device by maximizing use of on-chip memory: shared memory and caches (i.e., L1 cache and L2 cache available on devices of compute capability 2.x and higher, texture cache and constant cache available on all devices).\nShared memory is equivalent to a user-managed cache: The application explicitly allocates and accesses it. As illustrated in\nCUDA Runtime\n, a typical programming pattern is to stage data coming from device memory into shared memory; in other words, to have each thread of a block:\nLoad data from device memory to shared memory,\nSynchronize with all the other threads of the block so that each thread can safely read shared memory locations that were populated by different threads,\nProcess the data in shared memory,\nSynchronize again if necessary to make sure that shared memory has been updated with the results,\nWrite the results back to device memory.\nFor some applications (for example, for which global memory access patterns are data-dependent), a traditional hardware-managed cache is more appropriate to exploit data locality. As mentioned in\nCompute Capability 7.x\n,\nCompute Capability 8.x\nand\nCompute Capability 9.0\n, for devices of compute capability 7.x, 8.x and 9.0, the same on-chip memory is used for both L1 and shared memory, and how much of it is dedicated to L1 versus shared memory is configurable for each kernel call.\nThe throughput of memory accesses by a kernel can vary by an order of magnitude depending on access pattern for each type of memory. The next step in maximizing memory throughput is therefore to organize memory accesses as optimally as possible based on the optimal memory access patterns described in\nDevice Memory Accesses\n. This optimization is especially important for global memory accesses as global memory bandwidth is low compared to available on-chip bandwidths and arithmetic instruction throughput, so non-optimal global memory accesses generally have a high impact on performance.\n5.3.1.\nData Transfer between Host and Device\n\nApplications should strive to minimize data transfer between the host and the device. One way to accomplish this is to move more code from the host to the device, even if that means running kernels that do not expose enough parallelism to execute on the device with full efficiency. Intermediate data structures may be created in device memory, operated on by the device, and destroyed without ever being mapped by the host or copied to host memory.\nAlso, because of the overhead associated with each transfer, batching many small transfers into a single large transfer always performs better than making each transfer separately.\nOn systems with a front-side bus, higher performance for data transfers between host and device is achieved by using page-locked host memory as described in\nPage-Locked Host Memory\n.\nIn addition, when using mapped page-locked memory (\nMapped Memory\n), there is no need to allocate any device memory and explicitly copy data between device and host memory. Data transfers are implicitly performed each time the kernel accesses the mapped memory. For maximum performance, these memory accesses must be coalesced as with accesses to global memory (see\nDevice Memory Accesses\n). Assuming that they are and that the mapped memory is read or written only once, using mapped page-locked memory instead of explicit copies between device and host memory can be a win for performance.\nOn integrated systems where device memory and host memory are physically the same, any copy between host and device memory is superfluous and mapped page-locked memory should be used instead. Applications may query a device is\nintegrated\nby checking that the integrated device property (see\nDevice Enumeration\n) is equal to 1.\n5.3.2.\nDevice Memory Accesses\n\nAn instruction that accesses addressable memory (i.e., global, local, shared, constant, or texture memory) might need to be re-issued multiple times depending on the distribution of the memory addresses across the threads within the warp. How the distribution affects the instruction throughput this way is specific to each type of memory and described in the following sections. For example, for global memory, as a general rule, the more scattered the addresses are, the more reduced the throughput is.\nGlobal Memory\nGlobal memory resides in device memory and device memory is accessed via 32-, 64-, or 128-byte memory transactions. These memory transactions must be naturally aligned: Only the 32-, 64-, or 128-byte segments of device memory that are aligned to their size (i.e., whose first address is a multiple of their size) can be read or written by memory transactions.\nWhen a warp executes an instruction that accesses global memory, it coalesces the memory accesses of the threads within the warp into one or more of these memory transactions depending on the size of t\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 472000:476000]\nuda::pipeline\n\nIn the previous examples with\ncooperative_groups::wait\nand\ncuda::barrier\n, the kernel threads immediately wait for the data transfer to shared memory to complete. It is user’s responsibility to make sure that the increment on the arrival count does not exceed\n__mbarrier_maximum_count()\n.\n7.29.\nAsynchronous Data Copies using the Tensor Memory Accelerator (TMA)\n\nMany applications require movement of large amounts of data from and to global\n\nmemory. Often, the data is laid out in global memory as a multi-dimensional\n\narray with non-sequential data acess patterns. To reduce global memory usage,\n\nsub-tiles of such arrays are copied to shared memory before use in computations.\n\nThe loading and storing involves address-calculations that can be error-prone\n\nand repetitive. To offload these computations, Compute Capability 9.0 introduces the\n\nTensor Memory Accelerator (TMA). The primary goal of TMA is to provide an efficient\n\ndata transfer mechanism from global memory to shared memory for\n\nmulti-dimensional arrays.\nNaming\n. Tensor memory accelerator (TMA) is a broad term used to refer to the\n\nfeatures described in this section. For the purpose of forward-compatibility and\n\nto reduce discrepancies with the PTX ISA, the text in this section refers to TMA\n\noperations as either bulk-asynchronous copies or bulk tensor asynchronous\n\ncopies, depending on the specific type of copy used. The term “bulk” is used to\n\ncontrast these operations with the asynchronous memory operations described in\n\nthe previous sections.\nDimensions\n. TMA supports copying both one-dimensional and multi-dimensional\n\narrays (up to 5-dimensional). The programming model for\nbulk-asynchronous\n\ncopies\nof one-dimensional contiguous arrays is different from the programming\n\nmodel for\nbulk tensor asynchronous copies\nof multi-dimensional arrays. To\n\nperform a bulk tensor asynchronous copy of a multi-dimensional array, the\n\nhardware requires a\ntensor map\n.\n\nThis object describes the layout of the multi-dimensional array in global and\n\nshared memory. A tensor map is typically created on the host using the\ncuTensorMapEncode\n\nAPI\nand then transferred from host to device as a\nconst\nkernel parameter annotated\n\nwith\n__grid_constant__\n.\n\nThe tensor map is transferred from host to device as a\nconst\nkernel\n\nparameter annotated with\n__grid_constant__\n, and can be used on the device to\n\ncopy a tile of data between shared and global memory. In contrast, performing a\n\nbulk-asynchronous copy of a contiguous one-dimensional array does not require a\n\ntensor map: it can be performed on-device with a pointer and size parameter.\nSource and destination\n. The source and destination addresses of bulk-asynchronous copy\n\noperations can be in shared or global memory. The operations can read data from global to\n\nshared memory, write data from shared to global memory, and also copy from\n\nshared memory to\nDistributed Shared Memory\nof another block in the same cluster.\n\nIn addition, when in a cluster, a bulk-asynchronous operation can be specified as being\n\nmulticast. In this case, data can be transferred from global memory to the\n\nshared memory of multiple blocks within the cluster. The multicast feature is\n\noptimized for target architecture\nsm_90a\nand may have\nsignificantly reduced performance\non\n\nother targets. Hence, it is advised to be used with\ncompute architecture\nsm_90a\n.\nAsynchronous\n. Data transfers using TMA are\nasynchronous\n. This allows the initiating\n\nthread to continue computing while the hardware asynchronously copies the data.\nWhether the data transfer occurs asynchronously in practice is up to the hardware implementation and may change in the future\n.\n\nThere are several\ncompletion mechanisms\nthat bulk-asynchronous operations can use to signal that they have completed.\n\nWhen the operation reads from global to shared memory, any\n\nthread in the block can wait for the data to be readable in shared memory by\n\nwaiting on a\nShared Memory Barrier\n. When the bulk-asynchronous\n\noperation writes data from shared memory to global or distributed shared memory,\n\nonly the initiating thread can wait for the operation to have completed.\n\nThis is accomplished using a\nbulk async-group\nbased completion mechanism. A\n\ntable describing the completion mechanisms can be found below and in the\nPTX ISA\n.\nTable 6\nAsynchronous copies with possible source and destinations memory spaces and completion mechanisms. An empty cell indicates that a source-destination pair is not supported.\n\nDirection\nCompletion mechanism\nDestination\nSource\nAsychronous copy\nBulk-asynchronous copy (TMA)\nGlobal\nGlobal\nGlobal\nShared::cta\nBulk async-group\nShared::cta\nGlobal\nAsync-group, mbarrier\nMbarrier\nShared::cluster\nGlobal\nMbarrier (multicast)\nShared::cta\nShared::cluster\nMbarrier\nShared::cta\nShared::cta\n7.29.1.\nUsing TMA to transfer one-dimensional arrays\n\nThis section demonstrates how to write a simple kernel that read-modify-writes a\n\none-dimensional array using TMA. The size of a tensor is the number of elements along one\n\ndimension. All sizes must be greater than one. The stride is the number of bytes\n\nbetween elements of the same dimension. For instance, a\n4 x 4\nmatrix of\n\nintegers has sizes 4 and 4. Since it has 4 bytes per element, the strides are 4\n\nand 16 bytes. Due to alignment requirements, a\n4 x 3\nrow-major matrix of\n\nintegers must have strides of 4 and 16 bytes as well. Each row is padded with 4\n\nextra bytes to ensure that the start of the next row is aligned to 16 bytes. have a higher performance impact if any of the arguments is a unified memory pointer.\nThus, it is recommended to follow the following performance advice:\nWhen the physical location of unified memory
